A. County Council Open Session Minutes – October 6, 2026 
B. Accounts Payable Disbursement Report – Week Ending October 16, 2026 
C. Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) Request – School Resource Officer (SRO) – Sheriff’s Office 
 Request of the Sheriff’s Office to enter into a SRO MOU Implementation and Administration of 
Programs Involving Sheriff Deputies in Schools, by and between the County, Sheriff's Office and Board

 Request of Public Works to advertise Bid No. 2026-16, to request proposals for crusted stone, with a 
bid deadline of November 9, 2026

 Request of ALL4LOVE to provide a Letter of Support for its $1,700,000 Governor’s FY28 Capital 
Budget Request to redevelop The Warehouse, a permanent youth and community facility in Cambridge 
serving young people and families throughout the County 
6. Commendation  
A. Eastern Shore Innovation Center – Economic Development 
 Present a commendation to the Economic Development team for a decade of growth and excellence of

4. Brookview Bridge

3. Sewage Disposal System Acknowledgement

4. Juvenile Crime

 To discuss the following funding initiatives, recommended by the County Lobbying Firm, R&R Solutions,

1. Governor’s FY28 Capital Budget: $495,000 for improvements to the Corrections facility, including the 
replacement of heating and ventilation units in the main male housing unit, facility control center 
panels, the intercom, and the alert system, with a $45,000 in-kind match requirement

10. Action Items 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
7:30 p.m. 
A. Wheelchair Permit Process Discussion 
 Request of Chesapeake Housing Mission, Inc. to waive building permits for wheelchair ramps built by 
the organization 
 Recommendation of Planning & Zoning to maintain the current permitting and inspection requirements 
B. Letter of Opposition Request – Copper-Based Landlines  
 Request to submit a Letter of Opposition to the Federal Communications Commission regarding an 
application submitted by Verizon to discontinue copper-based infrastructure that supports Plain Old 
Telephone Services 
C. Letters of Support Request – Atlantic Menhaden  Conservation Efforts 
 Request to submit a Letter of Support to the Atlantic States Marine Fisheries Commission regarding the 
Draft Addendum II to Amendment 3 to the Interstate Fishery Management Plan for Atlantic Menhaden, 
which aims to increase conservation efforts that will support regional ecological health and the local 
seafood and recreational fishing economies 
D. Traffic Safety Committee Recommendations (TSC) 
 Request of the TSC to approve the following recommendations: 
1. Cloverdale School Road: install two additional 25MPH signs in between the North Dorchester 
High & Middle Schools, including "Fines Doubled" signs under each  
2. Oak Grove Road/Wheatley Road: install warning signs to alert drivers of oncoming traffic 
3. Bestpitch Ferry Road: verify the proposed locations and coordinate installation of the no-parking 
signs, subject to applicable regulations 
4. Galestown Reliance Road/Lakeshore Drive: send letters to the Maryland State Police and Sheriff’s 
Office formally requesting increased speed enforcement 
E. Poll Confirmation(s) 
 Consideration of the Council to confirm the following decision(s) in the interim of meetings, via poll: 
1. Approved the request of Emergency Medical Services to proceed with the purchase of cardiac 
monitor, at an annual cost of $89,431.78 over a 7-year period, through a Stryker financial plan, 
with the first payment funded by the FY27 Capital Budget, acknowledging the deadline of October 
15, 2026, by a 5 to 0 vote

County Council of Dorchester County 
Legislative Day No. 12 
Open Meeting Minutes 
October 6, 2026 
 
The County Council of Dorchester County convened into Open Session on October 6, 2026 at 
5:03 p.m., in Room 110 of the County Office Building, 501 Court Lane, Cambridge, Maryland 
21613.  A roll call of the members was conducted. Present in Room 110 were: George L. Pfeffer, 
Jr., President; Mike Detmer, Vice President; Rob Kramer, Jr.; William V. Nichols; and, Ricky C. 
Travers. Also present in Room 110 were: Jerry Jones, County Manager; Keith Benton, Deputy 
County Manager; Charles MacLeod, County Attorney; and, Irene Barnes, Executive Operations 
Manager. 
 
CLOSED SESSION  
 
Upon motion by Councilman Detmer, seconded by Councilman Kramer, the Council 
unanimously adjourned into Closed Session at 5:04 p.m., pursuant to the following sections of 
Title 3 of the General Provisions Article of the Maryland Annotated Code, §3-305(b)(1) to 
discuss the appointment, employment, assignment, promotion, discipline, demotion, 
compensation, removal, resignation, or performance evaluation of appointees, employees, or 
officials over whom it has jurisdiction; §3-305(b)(13) to comply with a specific constitutional, 
statutory, or judicially imposed requirement that prevents public disclosures about a particular 
proceeding or matter; and, §3-305(b)(7) to consult with counsel to obtain legal advice. The 
Written Statement for Closing a Meeting under the Open Meetings Act is attached hereto. 
 
RECONVENE INTO OPEN SESSION 
 
The County Council of Dorchester County convened into Open Session on October 6, 2026 at 
6:08 p.m., in Room 110 of the County Office Building, 501 Court Lane, Cambridge, Maryland 
21613.  Present in Room 110 were: George L. Pfeffer, Jr., President; Mike Detmer, Vice 
President; Rob Kramer, Jr.; William V. Nichols; and, Ricky C. Travers. Also present in Room 
110 were: Jerry Jones, County Manager; Keith Benton, Deputy County Manager; Charles 
MacLeod, County Attorney; and, Irene Barnes, Executive Operations Manager. 
 
INVOCATION AND PLEDGE OF ALLEGIANCE 
 
Councilman Detmer, led the invocation and the pledge of allegiance. 
 
CALL FOR ADDITIONS OR DELETIONS TO AGENDA 
 
There were no additions or deletions to the agenda. 
 
FINANCIAL REPORT – GENERAL FUND – OPERATING CASH ON HAND 
(ALLOCATED & UNALLOCATED) 
 
Councilman Pfeffer reported total as $40,982,231.06.

CLOSED SESSION SUMMARY 
 
The Council convened into a Closed Session at 5:04 p.m. on October 6, 2026 to discuss the 
following items pursuant to Title 3 of the General Provisions Article of the Maryland Annotated 
Code §3-305(b)(1), (13) and (7): 
 
1. Notifications of recent personnel changes within the County were provided. 
2. Personnel matters relating to specific individuals and positions; 
3. Received legal advice from the County Attorney regarding the following: 
a. Proposed disposition of a specific County-owned property   
b. Policies and procedures relating to specific fiscal matters  
c. Processes relating to a specific Planning & Zoning matter 
d. A specific State code relating to a fiscal matter 
e. A specific process relating to County projects 
f. A specific agreement relating to public safety 
g. A State code relating to a specific association 
h. A proposed participation agreement relating to a State settlement 
i. A concern relating to a specific organization

COMMENDATIONS 
 
LIFE SAVING EFFORTS 
 
The Council presented a Commendation to first responders for exceptional teamwork, courage, 
and dedication demonstrated during a complex motor vehicle crash. 
 
PROCLAMATIONS 
 
WHITE CANE AWARENESS DAY 
 
The Council presented a Proclamation declaring October 15, 2026 as White Cane Awareness 
Day to recognize the rights of blind people to move freely and independently with a cane or 
guide dog, which is affirmed by White Cane laws in all 50 states. 
 
PRESENTATIONS  
 
TOURISM 
 
Holly Gilpin, Tourism Director, presented a department update, including a FY25 State, 
Regional and Local Tourism Recap, marketing efforts and upcoming events.  
 
REAL PROPERTY NOTIFICATION SYSTEM 
 
Amy Craig, Clerk of the Court, Circuit Court, provided information on a new Real Property 
Notification System.  
 
DORCHESTER COUNTY PUBLIC LIBRARY (DCPL) 
 
Julie Kennedy, Assistant Director, DCPL; Hannah Smutny, Youth Services Coordinator, DCPL; 
and, Lanett Bagley, Hurlock Branch Manager, DCPL, provided information on the success of its 
Summer Reading Program, and future library happenings. 
 
ANNUAL TRANSPORTATION MEETING TOPICS  
 
Upon motion by Councilman Travers, seconded by Councilman Kramer, the Council 
unanimously agreed to the following list of State roadway concerns throughout the County to be 
discussed at the annual meeting with Maryland Department of Transportation officials on 
October 20, 2026: 
 
 West Side Connector 
 Hurlock to Linkwood Rail Line Reactivation  
 Boardwalk Under Malkus Bridge/Fishing Pier 
 Brookview Bridge  
 Waddell’s Corner/Route 331 Intersection

Dorchester County Council 
Open Session Minutes of October 6, 2026

 Main Street (Route 16) Truck Detour 
 
MARYLAND GENERAL ASSEMBLY 2027 LEGISLATIVE SESSION INITIATIVES 
 
Upon motion by Councilman Travers, seconded by Councilman Kramer, the Council 
unanimously agreed to remove the Workers Compensation for Volunteer Fire Companies 
initiative from the list of proposed Maryland General Assembly's 2027 Legislative Session 
initiatives to be discussed at the annual meeting with District 37 Legislators on October 20, 
2026. 
 
Upon motion by Councilman Detmer, seconded by Councilman Travers, the Council 
unanimously agreed to add a Payment In Lieu of Taxes discussion to the list. 
 
The Council agreed to submit the following list to the legislators for discussion: 
 
 Juvenile Crime 
 Archery Safety Zone  
 Sewage Disposal System Acknowledgement 
 Hotel Tax Rate Increase 
 Priority Preservation Area 
 Solar Payment In Lieu of Taxes 
 
Upon motion by Councilman Travers, seconded by Councilman Kramer, the Council 
unanimously adjourned into Legislative Session.  
 
LEGISLATIVE SESSION 
 
INTRODUCTION – ZONING AMENDMENT – ACCESSORY DWELLING UNITS 
 
Ms. Barnes said for introduction is an amendment to §155-50 Supplementary Use Regulations of 
the County Code to authorize and regulate Accessory Dwelling Units (ADUs) in conformance 
with Title 4, Subtitle 5 of the Land Use Article of the Annotated Code of Maryland. 
 
Ms. Webb said these amendments are being recommended to comply with State requirements, 
and provided a brief overview of the proposed changes.  
 
Upon motion by Councilman Travers, seconded by Councilman Nichols, the Council 
unanimously agreed to proceed with the publication of a public hearing. 
 
Upon motion by Councilman Travers, seconded by Councilman Detmer, the Council 
unanimously adjourned into Open Session.

BID AWARD RECOMMENDATION – COUNTY OWNED REAL PROPERTY – 
PLANNING & ZONING  
 
Upon motion by Councilman Kramer, seconded by Councilman Detmer, the Council 
unanimously agreed to consider the bids in one motion.  
 
Upon motion by Councilman Travers, seconded by Councilman Kramer the Council 
unanimously approved the recommendations of Susan Webb, Planning & Zoning Director, to 
award Bid No. 2026-10 to the most responsive bidders for the purchase of County-owned real 
properties: 
 
 6221 Mission Hill Road – $2,251 Michal Long  
 5314 Bucktown Road – $68,000 2 Daughters, LLC  
 7013 Butler Road – $1,000 Cynthia Morrison 
 Hoopers Neck Road – $5,165 Ridgeton Farms 
 2143 Wingate Bishops Head Road – $4,000 Richard Dawson 
 Jones Thicket Road – $505 Waller Property Services 
 2125 Wingate Bishops Head Road – $3,000 Richard Dawson 
 
Upon motion by Councilman Detmer, seconded by Councilman Kramer the Council 
unanimously approved the recommendations of Ms. Webb to rebid the remaining properties 
that were not sold.  
 
REQUEST TO SUBMIT – FY2027 PROGRAM OPEN SPACE (POS) ANNUAL 
PROGRAM – RECREATION & PARKS  
 
Upon motion by Councilman Travers, seconded by Councilman Kramer, the Council 
unanimously approved the recommendation of Duc Trieu, Recreation & Parks Director, to 
submit its FY27 POS Annual Program, including the following projects, to the Department of 
Natural Resources, with a 10% local match funded by the FY27 Contingency Fund: 
 
 School Street Athletic Complex ($15,000 local match) 
 Christ Rock Park Fencing/Field/Dugout ($8,000 local match) 
 Maple Dam Soft Launch ($5,000 local match) 
 
REQUEST TO PROCEED - HEATED VENT SYSTEM REPLACEMENT – 
CORRECTIONS 
 
Upon motion by Councilman Kramer, seconded by Councilman Detmer, the Council 
unanimously approved the request of Don Satterfield, Corrections Director, to proceed with the 
replacement of heated vent units 5, 6 and 7, using a current service agreement with Gannon’s

Dorchester County Council 
Open Session Minutes of October 6, 2026 
Page No. 7 
 
Refrigeration Services, Inc. at the cost of $88,556, funded by the FY27 Capital Budget: HVAC 
Units No. 5-7. 
 
BID AWARD RECOMMENDATION – CGE RAMP PAVEMENT REPAIR PROJECT – 
AIRPORT  
 
Upon motion by Councilman Detmer, seconded by Councilman Kramer, the Council 
unanimously approved the recommendation of Steve Nuwer, Airport to award Bid No. 2026-12 
to the sole bidder, David A. Bramble, Inc. at the cost of $64,351, for the CGE Ramp Pavement 
Repair Project, with 75% funded by a Maryland Aviation Administration grant, and 25% by the 
FY27 Capital Budget: Taxiway Repair.  
 
REQUEST TO ADVERTISE – COMMUNITY REINVESTMENT & REPAIR FUND 
(CRRF) GRANT CYCLE – CRRF COMMITTEE 
 
Upon motion by Councilman Travers, seconded by Councilman Detmer, the Council 
unanimously approved the request of Marilyn Williams, Grant Writer, Finance, on behalf of the 
CRRF Committee, to advertise Bid No. 2026-14 to initiate a new CRRF grant cycle, and invite 
community-based organizations to apply for financial support intended to benefit low-income 
communities and disproportionately impacted areas, while maintaining a balance within the fund 
for emergency community funding needs.  
 
Ms. Williams provided additional information. 
 
POOL SAFETY BARRIER DISCUSSION  
 
The Council discussed amending §155-50(A)(4)(d) Supplementary Use Regulations of the 
County Code requiring “a secure fence at least four feet in height shall enclose any in-ground 
swimming pool located within 200 feet of any adjoining property line or public way.” 
 
Jacob Van Schaik, Superior Pools, recommended introducing legislation to amend the Code to 
recognize an automatic/powered pool safety cover as an alternative pool safety barrier.  
 
Ms. Webb provided information on a new law passed by the State of Maryland in March 2026, 
and recommended replacing “fence” with “barrier” in the County Code for consistency with the 
new State law.  
 
Upon motion by Councilman Travers, seconded by Councilman Detmer, the Council directed 
staff to work with Mr. MacLeod to obtain additional information.   
 
QUARTERLY BOARD/COMMITTEE REVIEW 
 
The Council acknowledged that Dion Banks was sworn in by the Governor in June 2026 to 
fill the alternate vacancy on the Property Tax Assessment Appeals Board.

Upon motion by Councilman Detmer, seconded by Councilman Nichols, the Council 
unanimously reappointed Wylie Gray, Jr. and Scott Hanson to the Recreation & Parks

Upon motion by Councilman Detmer, seconded by Councilman Travers, the Council 
unanimously reappointed Dorothy Samonisky (second of three terms) and David Beverley 
(second of three terms) to the Burial Sites Preservation Board. 
 
Upon motion by Councilman Detmer, seconded by Councilman Kramer, the Council 
unanimously reappointed Derek Griffin (third/final term), Cherry Moaney (second of three

COUNTY MANAGER COMMENTS 
 
Mr. Jones and Mr. Ozman provided information on similar flooding concerns on Shorters Wharf, 
Hoopers Island and Elliott Island found in minutes from 1933. 
 
Mr. Jones highlighted that many of the same concerns were occurring 100 years ago.  
 
DEPUTY COUNTY MANAGER COMMENTS 
 
There we no comments.  
 
COUNCIL COMMENTS 
 
Councilman Detmer highlighted positive aspects of the County and what it offers, and spoke on 
the life of Robert Hanson.  
 
Councilman Travers asked Mr. Jones to work with the City of Cambridge to turn on the lights at 
the Franklin Street boat ramp. 
 
Councilman Pfeffer requested two items to be considered at the October 20, 2026 Council 
meeting: 1) letter of opposition to Verizon regarding the removal of copper lines; and, 2) letter of 
support in favor of conservation measures for Atlantic menhaden in the Chesapeake Bay. 
 
PUBLIC COMMENTS 
 
Norma Gray Bukowski, resident, expressed her appreciation for Elliotts Island being included in 
the NOAA transportation grant, and the assistance provided by Ms. Williams, Mr. Ozman, Mr. 
Jones and the Council.  
 
Andrew Bukowski, resident, spoke on workers compensation insurance for volunteer fire 
companies, and on concerns relating to juvenile crime. 
 
Sharon Smith, resident, thanked the Council and Mr. Jones for providing a public Action Pack, 
and spoke on items within the packet.

CRUSHED STONE
SEALED BIDS for Bid No. 2026-16 Supplying to the Department of Public Works, Crushed Stone for a 
two year period beginning November, 2026 and ending November 30, 2028 will be received at the 
Dorchester County Council Office, 501 Court Lane, Room 108, Cambridge, Maryland 21613 until 3:00

contract.
Questions Contact: Stephanie Newcomb, at 410-228-2920 or snewcomb@dorchestermd.gov
Bid Opening Date: 3:00 P.M. prevailing time, Monday, November 9, 2026
SPECIFICATIONS
Tonnage may vary due to weather conditions and economic conditions.
Tonnage by size is estimated per year to be:
AASHTO # 3 or MD #2
    600 Tons
AASHTO # 57 or MD #6
    400 Tons
WASHED AASHTO #8 OR MD # 7       
4,000 Tons
AASHTO # 10 or MD # 10
           1,000 Tons
MD CR6 or GASAB
            2,000 Tons
Stockpile Locations:
F.O.B. Shipping Point or Suppliers Location
Dorchester County Dept. Public Works, 5435 Handley Road, Cambridge, Maryland
DELIVERY SCHEDULE
Bidders shall be prepared to deliver daily quantities in a minimum amount equal to approximately 300 
tons.  Bidder shall be held responsible for the cost of any additional staff time needed to accommodate 
lesser daily delivery schedules.   Cost of providing additional county staff will be deducted from invoices.  
The successful bidder will be notified on the exact stockpile area and of the exact tonnage at each point 
at such time when the material is ordered.  The successful bidder will be required to begin delivery to any 
site within two weeks of notification, however, this should not be construed to mean that the Dorchester 
County Department of Public Works will begin to order within this period.

<!-- page 27 -->
Bid No. 2026-16 
GENERAL PROVISION MATERIAL SPECIFICATION
All material shall meet current Maryland State Highway Administration specifications for crushed stone 
except graduation of # 10 should contain a minimum of 9% - 200 mesh material. 
A certificate of inspection, by a reputable testing company, will be required if so requested by the 
Dorchester County Public Works Director.
BIDDING FORM
All bids shall be submitted on the “Standard Bidding Form” for Dorchester County Department of Public 
Works.
TIME FOR FILING/BID OPENING
All sealed bids shall be submitted on or before 3:00 P.M., Monday, November 9, 2026 to the Dorchester
County Council Office BID BOX; County Office Building, 501 Court Lane, Room 108, Cambridge, 
Maryland, 21613.  No bids will be accepted after 3:00 P.M., Monday, November 9, 2026. Envelopes
should be clearly marked Bid No. 2026-16 SUPPLYING THE DORCHESTER COUNTY DEPT.
PUBLIC WORKS CRUSHED STONE.
Bids will be opened at that time and publicly read thereafter in Room 110.
Vendors are responsible for assuring that their bids are delivered to the specified location before the
deadline for receipt of bids, including those delivered by U.S. Postal Service.  
Oral, fax or E-mail bids will not be accepted.  Bids, request for withdraws, and modifications not received 
by the time and place indicated are late and may not be considered.
Bids will then be reviewed by the Public Works Director and a recommendation will be submitted to the 
Dorchester County for their approval.
BOND
A bid bond equal to 5% of the total bid shall accompany each bid.  A performance bond shall be required 
of the bidder who is awarded the contract in the amount of the total amount bid.
RESPONSIBILITY OF BIDDER
Failure to comply with any of the requirements included in this proposal will be deemed just cause for 
cancellation of the contract by the Dorchester County Department of Public Works and for the pro-rata 
assessment against the performance bond for having the contract quantities supplied by others.
TAX EXEMPTION
Dorchester County, being a political subdivision, is exempt by law, from applicable excise and sales 
taxes.  The successful bidder must have the ability to bill the County for fuels free of excise and sales 
taxes.

ALL4LOVE acquired The Warehouse in 2025 and has since leveraged local government, philanthropic, and community contributions to
complete initial redevelopment and activate portions of the facility. The remaining construction will expand dedicated space for youth
recreation, mentorship, educational enrichment, intervention services, and community partnerships. The project will strengthen
youth-serving infrastructure in Dorchester County.
Complete the remaining redevelopment of The Warehouse; expand access to safe, structured youth development opportunities;
strengthen connections between school-based and community-based supports; and establish a sustainable community asset serving young
people and families throughout Dorchester County.

Governor's FY2028 Capital Budget.

<!-- page 33 -->
[Date]
The Honorable Wes Moore
Governor of Maryland
100 State Circle
Annapolis, Maryland 21401
RE: Support for ALL4LOVE - FY2028 Capital Budget Request
Dear Governor Moore:
On behalf of Dorchester County Government, I am pleased to express our strong support for 
ALL4LOVE's request for $1.7 million through the Governor's FY2028 Capital Budget to support the 
continued redevelopment and completion of The Warehouse in Cambridge, Maryland.
ALL4LOVE has grown into an important partner in the network of organizations and public institutions 
serving young people and families throughout Dorchester County. Through mentoring, youth athletics, 
school-based student supports, prevention and intervention services, enrichment opportunities, and 
community engagement, the organization is reaching young people across our county and helping connect 
families to relationships, resources, and opportunities that support long-term success.
That work is particularly important in a rural county such as Dorchester. Our geography, transportation 
barriers, concentrated areas of poverty, and limited availability of dedicated youth-serving spaces can 
make it difficult for young people and families to consistently access opportunities and support. 
Investments in strong community-based organizations and permanent infrastructure can help address 
these challenges while strengthening the capacity of our community to serve its own residents.
The Warehouse represents an opportunity to create that type of lasting infrastructure. Once fully 
developed, the facility will provide dedicated space for recreation, mentoring, educational and leadership 
programming, family engagement, intervention services, community partnerships, and other opportunities 
for young people. Although located in Cambridge, The Warehouse will serve young people and families 
from throughout Dorchester County and complement the work already taking place through schools, local 
government, recreation, public safety, nonprofit organizations, and other community partners.
ALL4LOVE has also demonstrated its ability to leverage resources from multiple sectors to move this 
project forward. Local government, philanthropy, foundations, businesses, individual donors, and 
community members have already contributed to the redevelopment of The Warehouse. Rather than 
waiting for the entire project to be funded, ALL4LOVE has completed portions of the facility and begun 
putting those spaces to use for young people. A $1.7 million State capital investment would build upon 
that existing commitment and provide the resources necessary to complete the remaining redevelopment.
The County recognizes that challenges facing young people cannot be addressed by any single institution. 
Schools, government, law enforcement, families, nonprofit organizations, philanthropy, and community 
partners each have a role to play. Facilities like The Warehouse create a place where those efforts can 
intersect and where young people can remain connected to trusted adults and opportunities beyond the 
boundaries of any one program or system.
For these reasons, Dorchester County Government strongly supports ALL4LOVE's request for $1.7 
million in the Governor's FY2028 Capital Budget. State investment in The Warehouse would help create 
a permanent community asset, leverage significant local and private investment, and expand the 
infrastructure available to support young people and families throughout Dorchester County.
Thank you for your consideration and for your continued investment in Maryland's rural communities.
Sincerely,
[Name]
[Title]
Dorchester County Government

Re: Dorchester County Annual Transportation Meeting 
 
Dear Mr. Crampton: 
 
On behalf of the Dorchester County Council, I respectfully submit the following topics for discussion 
ahead of the Annual Transportation Meeting scheduled on October 20, 2026. 
 
• West Side Connector: Connection from the Route 343 bypass to Chesapeake Drive to help reduce 
traffic congestion and relieve pressure on Washington Street 
• Hurlock-Linkwood Rail Corridor: Continue improvements of the corridor to advance efforts toward 
reactivation of the rail line, supporting industrial growth and regional freight movement 
• Boardwalk under Malkus Bridge/Fishing Pier: Repairs and maintenance of the boardwalk leading 
under the Malkus Bridge, along with details pertaining to the construction of a new fishing pier 
• Brookview Bridge: Upgrades to improve the bridge’s structural capacity, safety, and long-term 
reliability for commercial and passenger vehicles 
• Waddells Corner/Route 331 Intersection: Intersection improvements that enhance safety, traffic 
operations, and long-term functionality 
• Route 16 – Main Street, East New Market: Truck detour, routing landfill truck traffic to Route 392, 
then over to the landfill 
 
Thank you for your continued partnership, and dedication in maintaining roadways and bridges within 
Dorchester County.

Legislative Initiatives 
 
Local Government Article  
§20-405. Tax Rates. 
 
Request of the City of Cambridge to amend §20-405, of the Local Government Article of 
the Annotated Code of Maryland, entitled “Tax Rates.” 
 
On behalf of the City of Cambridge, the Council respectfully requests an amendment to §20-405, 
of the Local Government Article of the Annotated Code of Maryland, entitled “Tax Rates,” to 
increase the Hotel Tax rate from 5% to 6% in Dorchester County.  
 
This amendment will provide additional revenue to be invested in economic development 
initiatives, tourism and destination marketing efforts designed to support local businesses, attract 
visitors, encourage investment, and strengthen the local economy. 
 
Proposed Amendment: 
 
Amend §20-405 to the following,  
 
(b) The hotel rental tax rate may not exceed: 
 
(4) 5% in Calvert County, Carroll County, Charles County, Dorchester County, 
Frederick County, St. Mary’s County, and Somerset County; 
 
(5) 6% in Wicomico County, AND DORCHESTER COUNTY; and

<!-- page 40 -->
Natural Resources Article 
§10-410. Restrictions on Hunting Wildlife Generally. 
 
Request of the Maryland Bowhunters Society to amend §10-410 of the Natural Resources 
Article of the Annotated Code of Maryland, entitled “Restrictions on Hunting Wildlife 
Generally.” 
 
On behalf of the Maryland Bowhunters Society, the Council respectfully requests an amendment 
to §10-410, of the Natural Resources Article of the Annotated Code of Maryland, entitled 
“Restrictions on Hunting Wildlife Generally,” to reduce the existing 150-yard archery deer 
hunting safety zone to 50 yards, while retaining the existing 150-yard standard for firearm 
hunting. 
 
Archery deer hunting is an established component of Maryland’s deer-management program. 
Because archery is a quiet, short-range method of hunting, it can provide additional opportunities 
to manage deer in areas where firearm hunting may be impractical because of development, 
population density, property configuration or other land-use considerations. Maryland’s 
Department of Natural Resources has specifically noted that reduced archery safety zones can 
provide hunters access to additional deer habitat, including in urban and suburban areas, while 
reporting that it has not received a significant number of complaints associated with the reduced 
50-yard zones where they are established. 
 
For private landowners, archery can also provide an additional means of managing deer on 
properties where firearm hunting may be difficult or unavailable. A 50-yard archery safety zone 
would not eliminate a safety buffer around occupied structures; rather, it would establish a 
different safety standard for archery than for firearms, consistent with the approach already 
authorized in numerous Maryland jurisdictions. 
 
Proposed Amendment: 
 
Amend §20-405 to the following,  
 
(g) 
(3) 
(i) For archery hunters in Allegany County, Baltimore City, Calvert County, 
Carroll County, Cecil County, DORCHESTER COUNTY, Frederick County, 
Garrett County, Harford County, Montgomery County, St. Mary’s County, 
Washington County, Worcester County, or Wicomico County, the safety zone 
described in paragraph (1) of this subsection extends for 50-yards from a dwelling 
house, residence, church, or any other building or camp occupied by human 
beings. 
 
 
 
5

<!-- page 41 -->
Real Property Article 
§10-702. Single Family Residential Real Property Disclosure Requirements. 
 
Request to amend §10-702 of the Real Property Article of the Annotated Code of 
Maryland, entitled “Single Family Residential Real Property Disclosure Requirements.” 
The County Council of Dorchester County respectfully requests an amendment to §10-702, of 
the Real Property Article of the Annotated Code of Maryland to require a purchaser of real 
property to receive and acknowledge a standardized document identifying the type of sewage 
disposal system serving, or intended to serve, the property and any known responsibilities 
associated with that system. 
The proposed amendment would apply to both improved and unimproved real property when the 
property is served by, will be served by, or is otherwise subject to obligations associated with an 
individual or shared sewage disposal system. 
The intent of this amendment is to ensure that purchasers are provided clear and consistent 
information concerning sewage disposal systems and any associated responsibilities at the time 
of property transfer, including circumstances in which the existing residential disclosure 
requirements do not apply. 
Concerns: 
The County Council is concerned that the existing exemption for “a sale of unimproved real 
property” under §10-702, may allow a purchaser of a vacant lot to acquire property that is 
subject to significant sewage-system responsibilities without receiving a specific 
acknowledgment of those responsibilities at settlement. This concern is particularly relevant to 
properties that are, or are intended to be, served by a shared or community sewage disposal 
system, including systems associated with a Bay Restoration Fund-funded project. A purchaser 
may acquire an unimproved parcel with future obligations relating to the shared system, 
including operation, maintenance, repair, replacement, connection, or other costs or 
responsibilities. 
The Maryland Real Estate Commission has recently updated the required residential 
disclosure/disclaimer form to include “Sewage Disposal – Type of System Options.” However, 
the existing disclosure process does not address the concern presented by the exemption for 
unimproved real property. 
A separate, standardized acknowledgment would provide a mechanism to ensure that purchasers 
of both improved and unimproved property receive specific information concerning the sewage 
system and any associated responsibilities. 
Proposed Amendments: 
 
Update "disclosure or disclaimer statement" to "disclosure, disclaimer statement, or 
acknowledgement" throughout the code 
 
6

<!-- page 42 -->
(b) 
(2) This section does not apply to: 
(vii) A sale of unimproved real property, EXCEPT AS PROVIDED IN 
SUBSECTION (n) OF THIS SECTION. 
(n)  
(1) BEFORE OR AT THE TIME OF SETTLEMENT OF A SALE OF 
REAL PROPERTY THAT IS SERVED BY, WILL BE SERVED BY, OR IS 
SUBJECT TO AN OBLIGATION TO CONNECT TO OR PARTICIPATE IN A 
SHARED SEWAGE DISPOSAL SYSTEM, THE VENDOR SHALL PROVIDE TO 
THE PURCHASER A STANDARDIZED SEWAGE DISPOSAL SYSTEM 
ACKNOWLEDGMENT DEVELOPED BY THE STATE REAL ESTATE 
COMMISSION IN CONSULTATION WITH THE MARYLAND DEPARTMENT 
OF THE ENVIRONMENT. 
(2) THE ACKNOWLEDGMENT SHALL INCLUDE, AS APPLICABLE: 
(i) THE TYPE OF SEWAGE DISPOSAL SYSTEM SERVING OR 
INTENDED TO SERVE THE PROPERTY; 
(ii) WHETHER THE SEWAGE DISPOSAL SYSTEM IS AN INDIVIDUAL 
OR SHARED SYSTEM; 
(iii) WHETHER THE PROPERTY IS CURRENTLY CONNECTED TO 
THE SYSTEM OR IS INTENDED TO CONNECT TO THE SYSTEM; 
(iv) ANY KNOWN OPERATION, MAINTENANCE, REPAIR, 
REPLACEMENT, OR CONNECTION RESPONSIBILITIES OF THE 
PROPERTY OWNER; 
(v) ANY KNOWN FEES, ASSESSMENTS, OR OTHER FINANCIAL 
OBLIGATIONS ASSOCIATED WITH THE SEWAGE DISPOSAL 
SYSTEM; 
(vi) ANY RECORDED EASEMENT, COVENANT, AGREEMENT, OR 
OTHER INSTRUMENT AFFECTING THE PROPERTY RELATING TO 
THE SEWAGE DISPOSAL SYSTEM; AND 
(vii) ANY OTHER INFORMATION THAT THE STATE REAL ESTATE 
COMMISSION AND THE MARYLAND DEPARTMENT OF THE 
ENVIRONMENT DETERMINE IS NECESSARY TO INFORM A 
PURCHASER OF RESPONSIBILITIES ASSOCIATED WITH THE 
SEWAGE DISPOSAL SYSTEM. 
(3) THE PURCHASER SHALL DATE AND SIGN THE ACKNOWLEDGMENT 
TO CONFIRM RECEIPT OF THE INFORMATION REQUIRED UNDER THIS 
SUBSECTION. 
(4) THE ACKNOWLEDGMENT SHALL BE INCLUDED IN OR ATTACHED TO 
THE CONTRACT OF SALE OR SETTLEMENT DOCUMENTS. 
(5) THE ACKNOWLEDGMENT REQUIRED UNDER THIS SUBSECTION 
DOES NOT CONSTITUTE A WARRANTY BY THE VENDOR CONCERNING 
THE CONDITION, PERFORMANCE, CAPACITY, OR FUTURE COST OF THE 
SEWAGE DISPOSAL SYSTEM. 
(6) THE STATE REAL ESTATE COMMISSION, IN CONSULTATION WITH 
THE MARYLAND DEPARTMENT OF THE ENVIRONMENT, SHALL ADOPT 
REGULATIONS ESTABLISHING THE FORM AND REQUIREMENTS OF THE

8

<!-- page 44 -->
Opposition 
Tax – Property  
§6-306. County Tax Rate in Certain Other Municipal Corporations. 
 
The County Council of Dorchester County respectfully opposes a proposed amendment, by 
officials of the City of Cambridge, to §6-306, of the Tax – Property Article of the Annotated 
Code of Maryland, entitled “County Tax Rate in Certain Other Municipal Corporations,” to 
update §6-306(c), below, from “may” to “shall” 
 
(c) The governing body of the County shall meet and discuss with the 
governing body of any municipal corporation in the County the County 
property tax rate to be set for assessments of property in the municipal 
corporation as provided in this section. After the meeting if a municipal 
corporation performs services or programs instead of similar county services 
or programs, the governing body of the county may grant a tax setoff to the 
municipal corporation. 
 
County Opposition to Proposed Amendment 
 
The County Council respectfully opposes this amendment for the following reasons:  
 
• Preserves County fiscal discretion: Changing “may” to “shall” could turn a discretionary 
tax-setoff decision into a mandatory obligation, potentially requiring the County to 
reduce its tax revenue regardless of its financial circumstances. 
• No statutory formula for the amount of the setoff: The law provides factors the County 
may consider, but does not establish a formula for calculating a mandatory setoff. “Shall” 
could create a legal obligation without defining how much the county must provide. 
• Allows case-by-case consideration of municipal services: Municipalities provide different 
services at different levels and costs. The current language allows the County to 
determine whether a particular municipality's services actually warrant a setoff and at 
what level. 
• Preserves the negotiation process established by the statute: Sections (f) through (i) 
require financial information to be exchanged, representatives to meet, the County to 
explain its proposed setoff, and the municipality to have an opportunity to testify. 
Mandatory language could undermine this individualized negotiation process by making 
the ultimate outcome predetermined. 
• Avoids shifting County revenues without corresponding County control: A mandatory 
setoff could require the County to surrender property-tax revenue based on municipal 
decisions about what services to provide, even though the County does not control the 
municipality's service choices, costs, or spending priorities. 
 
 
9

<!-- page 45 -->
Topics of Discussion 
Juvenile Crime 
 
The County Council requests continued evaluation of potential legislative reforms, including 
improvements to detention and court jurisdiction criteria, enhanced intervention and 
rehabilitation services, and better coordination among the juvenile justice system, law 
enforcement, and community-based providers, to promote accountability, reduce recidivism, and 
protect public safety. 
 
During the 2026 legislative session, the General Assembly enacted changes to Maryland's 
juvenile justice laws, including Senate Bill 323, cross-filed with House Bill 409, entitled, 
“Juvenile Court - Jurisdiction, Detention, and Confinement (Youth Charging Reform Act)” for 
the purpose of altering the jurisdiction of the juvenile court by repealing provisions specifying 
that the juvenile court does not have jurisdiction over a child alleged to have committed any 
crime punishable by life imprisonment; requiring an intake officer to authorize detention for a 
child that is at least 16 years old and is accused of certain crimes; altering and establishing 
certain provisions relating to the detention, confinement, and transportation of certain children; 
etc.  
 
While these changes represent developments in the State's approach to juvenile crime, continued 
review is warranted to assess implementation and effectiveness, particularly with respect to 
repeat and serious juvenile offenses, as well as additional legislation and amendments to address 
gaps during implementation.  
 
 
10

<!-- page 46 -->
Solar Development & Agricultural Preservation 
Natural Resources/Public Utilities/Land Use - State review and local siting authority 
 
Request State legislation to provide counties stronger authority over the location of utility-
scale solar energy generating facilities, particularly on productive agricultural land and 
within designated Priority Preservation Areas. 
 
The County Council of Dorchester County respectfully requests legislation that provides 
meaningful local participation and greater authority in decisions concerning the siting of utility-
scale solar facilities. The legislation should recognize adopted comprehensive plans, agricultural 
preservation policies, local zoning, and the long term public investment in preserving farmland, 
while maintaining a workable process for renewable energy development. 
 
Concerns: 
 
Dorchester County supports responsible renewable energy development but is concerned that 
large solar installations may permanently or substantially displace productive farmland, fragment 
agricultural districts, and undermine land preservation efforts. State-level approvals that do not 
give sufficient weight to local land-use decisions may limit the County’s ability to protect 
agricultural resources and direct development to suitable locations. 
 
Proposed Amendment: 
 
Authorize counties to establish enforceable siting standards for utility-scale solar projects, 
including restrictions or heightened review for prime and productive agricultural soils and 
designated Priority Preservation Areas; require meaningful consideration of local comprehensive 
plans and zoning in State approval proceedings; and establish a clear mechanism for County 
findings and participation before a project is approved. Any proposal should address existing 
State preemption and applicable Public Service Commission authority. 
 
Proposed statutory language and placement to be determined in consultation with County legal 
counsel and the legislative delegation. 
 
 
11

<!-- page 47 -->
Priority Preservation Area Protections 
Agriculture/Land Use - Priority Preservation Areas 
 
Request legislation strengthening State protections for designated Priority Preservation 
Areas and authorizing counties to restrict incompatible development that would undermine 
agricultural preservation investments. 
 
The County Council of Dorchester County respectfully requests strengthened statutory 
protections for Priority Preservation Areas (PPAs) established through local agricultural 
preservation planning. Counties should have clear authority to adopt and enforce land-use 
protections that maintain the agricultural purpose of these areas and protect public expenditures 
and long-term preservation commitments. 
 
Concerns: 
 
Priority Preservation Areas are intended to support the continued viability of agriculture and 
focus preservation efforts. Incompatible large-scale development can fragment farmland, create 
land use conflicts, and reduce the effectiveness of public and private investments in agricultural 
preservation. Without adequate State support for local restrictions, designation alone may not 
provide meaningful protection. 
 
Proposed Amendment: 
 
Expressly authorize counties to impose reasonable restrictions on nonagricultural, incompatible 
development within designated PPAs; require State permitting and approval agencies to consider 
PPA designation and documented preservation investments; and clarify that local protective 
measures adopted under this authority may be enforced notwithstanding otherwise conflicting 
State siting provisions, to the extent expressly provided by the General Assembly. 
 
Proposed statutory language and placement to be determined in consultation with County legal 
counsel and the legislative delegation. 
12

The County Manager and Department Heads met with the County's new lobbying firm R&R Solutions. Each department provided a list
of needed capital improvement projects over the next five years. This project was identified as potentially qualifying for state funding.
The Dorchester County Department of Corrections oversees the maintenance and operations of the Dorchester County Detention Center
(the Detention Center), and is responsible for the health and well being of those incarcerated there. The Center houses convicted
incarcerated individuals as well as persons awaiting trial with an average daily population of between 120 to 225 inmates. Originally
constructed in 1991, the thirty-five year old building is in need of both mechanical and public safety systems upgrades.
Funding would be used to replace two HV units in the male main housing unit and to replace the facility control center panels, the
intercom, and the alert system. All of these systems are crucial for the safety of staff and the incarcerated individuals housed at the
Center.
Approval for R&R Solutions to submit this to the State for consideration in the Governor's budget.
R&R Solutions will work with the Governor's Office and General Assembly for consideration of inclusion in the state budget.

The County Manager and Department Heads met with the County's new lobbying firm R&R Solutions. Each department provided a list
of needed capital improvement projects over the next five years. This project was identified as potentially qualifying for state funding.
The Recreation Center serves as a hub for community recreation and engagement, housing the County’s Recreation and Parks offices,
indoor recreational programs, multipurpose athletic courts, and classroom space for the Boys and Girls Club. The facility provides
valuable opportunities for residents, particularly youth, to participate in recreational, athletic, and educational activities in a welcoming
and accessible environment.
Significant building improvements are needed to ensure the facility can continue to serve the community effectively. The roof, which is
original to the building’s construction in 1975, has deteriorated to the point that leaks occur during the winter months. Previous efforts to
apply a protective coating have been ineffective, leading to the decision that a total roof replacement is necessary. Additionally, the
facility’s restrooms are outdated and do not meet current ADA accessibility requirements. Addressing these deficiencies is essential to
protecting the building, improving its functionality, and ensuring equitable access for all users.
The total estimated construction cost for these improvements is $200,000.
To submit this request to the legislators at the Delegation meeting.
Obtain approval from State delegation to introduce LBI.
If the Delegation agrees to introduce the legislation, R&R Solutions will work with them and other members of the General Assembly
for consideration of the inclusion in the State Budget

DRAFT 
The Honorable Johnny Mautz 
Maryland State Senate  
8695 Commerce Dr. 
Easton, MD 21601 
The Honorable Tom Hutchinson 
Maryland House of Delegates 
308 Lowe House Office Building 
6 Bladen Street 
Annapolis, MD 21401 
The Honorable Chris Adams 
Maryland House of Delegates 
405 Lowe House Office Building 
6 Bladen Street 
Annapolis, MD 21401 
Senator Mautz and Delegates Hutchinson and Adams, 
On behalf of Dorchester County, I am writing to request your sponsorship of a $150,000 Legislative Bond 
Initiative (LBI) to support critical improvements to the Dorchester County Recreation Center, located at 
515 Leonards Lane in Cambridge, Maryland. The requested funding would support the planning, design, 
construction, repair, renovation, site improvements, and capital equipping necessary to preserve and 
enhance this important community facility. 
The Recreation Center serves as a hub for community recreation and engagement, housing the County’s 
Recreation and Parks offices, indoor recreational programs, multipurpose athletic courts, and classroom 
space for the Boys and Girls Club. The facility provides valuable opportunities for residents, particularly 
youth, to participate in recreational, athletic, and educational activities in a welcoming and accessible 
environment. 
Significant building improvements are needed to ensure the facility can continue to serve the community 
effectively. The roof, which is original to the building’s construction in 1975, has deteriorated to the point 
that leaks occur during the winter months. Previous efforts to apply a protective coating have been 
ineffective, leading to the decision that a total roof replacement is necessary. Additionally, the facility’s 
restrooms are outdated and do not meet current ADA accessibility requirements. Addressing these 
deficiencies is essential to protecting the building, improving its functionality, and ensuring equitable 
access for all users. 
The total estimated construction cost for these improvements is $200,000. Dorchester County has 
committed $50,000 in matching funds and respectfully requests an additional $150,000 through the 
Legislative Bond Initiative to complete this important project. 
Thank you for your consideration and continued support of Dorchester County. We would welcome the 
opportunity to host you at the Recreation Center for a tour and to discuss the proposed improvements 
and their benefits to our community if you would like to learn more about the project. 
Sincerely,

Recommendation for the request to waive the permitting process for wheelchair ramps submitted by CHM
The Planning & Zoning Department supports providing safe and accessible accommodations for 
individuals with disabilities. However, residential ramps must comply with applicable building codes and 
requirements related to structural safety, zoning setbacks, wells, septic systems, and Critical Area 
regulations. The Department recommends maintaining permitting and inspection requirements (no permit 
fees) to ensure these structures are safely constructed and appropriately located while supporting 
homeowners' accessibility needs.

Subject: ResidenƟal Handicap Ramps
£
In Maryland, residenƟal handicap ramps for private single-family homes are not directly governed by the Code
of Maryland RegulaƟons (COMAR). Instead, COMAR mandates the Maryland State Accessibility Code (COMAR
05.02.02), which primarily applies to public buildings, commercial faciliƟes, and mulƟ-family housing (4+ units). 
However, private residenƟal ramps must comply with local county building codes, which almost universally
adopt the InternaƟonal ResidenƟal Code (IRC), speciﬁcally SecƟon R311.8. While the Americans with
DisabiliƟes Act (ADA) guidelines are not legally mandated for private homes, building inspectors and
contractors heavily rely on them as the safety standard. 
The core building requirements for a safe, code-compliant residenƟal ramp in Maryland include:
 Slope, Rise & FooƟngs
Maximum Running Slope: The standard rule is a 1:12 slope raƟo. For every 1 inch of verƟcal rise, you
must have 12 inches (1 foot) of ramp length.
Maximum Cross Slope: The side-to-side slope cannot exceed 1:48 (2%) to prevent wheelchairs from
Ɵpping.
Maximum Single Run: A single ramp secƟon cannot rise more than 30 inches before requiring a ﬂat
landing.
FooƟng Depth & Diameter: 24" in depth x 8" diameter minimum and increases with larger tributary
loads.
If the landing height exceeds 30" other codes are in eﬀect such as speciﬁc guard and fall protecƟon codes.
These ramps need to inspected to ensure that they comply with code and are structurally sound.
Permits are also required due to other agencies that must review these structures for the setbacks, etc.
Ex: Health Dept requires a 30' setback from wells, 15' setback from sepƟc tanks and no structures permiƩed in
the sepƟc reserve areas.
       Zoning with setbacks from property lines, right aways, shared driveways and roads.
       CriƟcal Areas

Letter of Opposition
On September 9, 2026, Verizon submitted an application to the Federal Communications Commission to
discontinue copper-based infrastructure that support Plain Old Telephone Services (POTS), effective on or after
November 30, 2026, pending FCC approval. Concerns have been expressed, particularly in rural areas, on how this
may impact landlines, 911 access, alarms, medical alert devices, or other essential communications, and whether
each affected location has a workable replacement.

SECTION 63.71 APPLICATION OF VERIZON 
 
I.  
INTRODUCTION. 
With this Application,1 Verizon seeks authority to discontinue Plain Old Telephone 
Service (“POTS”) served by time-division multiplexing (“TDM”) technology over copper in 
portions of California, Connecticut, the District of Columbia, Illinois, Maryland, Massachusetts, 
Michigan, New York, Pennsylvania, and West Virginia.  Currently, Verizon’s network could 
serve over 28 million locations in these states with POTS.  However, the overwhelming majority 
of consumers from these states who could subscribe to POTS service from Verizon do not—only 
approximately 750,000 residential and business locations, or 2.7%, still subscribe to legacy voice 
services.  Copper infrastructure is too slow to meet modern needs, many decades old, costly to 
maintain, and vulnerable to damage and theft.  As detailed below, granting this Application will 
advance the Build America Agenda by allowing Verizon to direct investments toward the high-
speed networks that Americans want and away from maintaining the legacy copper networks that 
they are abandoning, while ensuring that consumers continue to have reliable access to voice 
 
1 The FCC Registration Number for this application is 0003628971.

<!-- page 69 -->
3 
service and 911.  Grant of this Application also is consistent with Commission and Wireline 
Competition Bureau (“Bureau”) precedent.   
Specifically, with this Application, Verizon seeks to discontinue copper-based residential 
and business legacy voice service in the nine states listed above and in the District of Columbia 
at locations where existing POTS customers can obtain from Verizon—or at least one of ten 
third parties, including AT&T, T-Mobile, Comcast, and Charter—one or more of the following 
facilities-based replacement voice services: (i) interconnected Voice over Internet Protocol 
(“VoIP”) provisioned over broadband using fiber or cable (i.e., coaxial cable or hybrid fiber-
coaxial cable); (ii) Verizon fixed wireless voice replacement services that enable legacy POTS 
telephone equipment to operate over Verizon’s wireless network;2 or (iii) mobile wireless 
service.3  These replacement services from Verizon and other well-established providers are the 
services that Verizon relies on in this Application to meet the requirements still in effect until the 
new Network Modernization Order rules the Commission adopted earlier this year take effect.4  
As detailed below, Verizon’s fiber-based voice service satisfies the Alternative Options Test,5 
and Verizon’s fixed wireless voice replacement and mobile wireless service—as well as the 
other replacements on which Verizon relies—satisfy the Adequate Replacement Test.6  By 
 
2 Verizon seeks approval based on its own fixed wireless service; it does not rely on third-party 
fixed wireless services in this Application. 
3 “Discontinuance area” refers to locations within the scope of this Application.   
4 See Reducing Barriers to Network Improvements and Service Changes; Accelerating Network 
Modernization, WC Docket Nos. 25-209, 25-208, Report and Order, FCC 26-19, App. A – Final 
Rules (rel. Mar. 27, 2026) (“Network Modernization Order”). 
5 See 47 C.F.R. § 63.71(f)(2)(ii).  Verizon relies on the Alternative Options Test for expediency 
because of the simpler showing required.   
6 See 47 C.F.R. § 63.71(a), (f)(2)(i); see also id. § 63.602(b) (permitting applicants to show that 
“one or more replacement service(s) satisfies all of the following criteria”).  In the Network 
Modernization Order, the Commission replaced the Alternative Options Test and Adequate 
Replacement Test with “one simple rule” allowing applications for discontinuance that identify

<!-- page 70 -->
4 
showing that locations in the discontinuance area have an available replacement that meets the 
Commission’s requirements for streamlined treatment, we ensure that the 2.7% of Verizon 
subscribers who still receive copper-based POTS in the ten jurisdictions at issue will be well-
served.    
Therefore, the following Verizon entities: Citizens Telecommunications Company of 
California Inc.; Citizens Telecommunications Company of Illinois; Citizens 
Telecommunications Company of New York, Inc. d/b/a Frontier Communications of New York; 
Citizens Telecommunications Company of West Virginia d/b/a Frontier Communications of 
West Virginia; Commonwealth Telephone Company LLC d/b/a Frontier Communications

Inc.; Frontier Communications of AuSable Valley, Inc.; Frontier Communications of 
Breezewood, LLC; Frontier Communications of Canton, LLC; Frontier Communications of the 
Carolinas LLC; Frontier Communications of Depue, Inc.; Frontier Communications of Illinois, 
Inc.; Frontier Communications of Lakeside, Inc.; Frontier Communications of Lakewood, LLC; 
Frontier Communications of Michigan, Inc.; Frontier Communications of Mt. Pulaski, Inc.; 
Frontier Communications of New York, Inc.; Frontier Communications of Orion, Inc.; Frontier 
Communications of Oswayo River, LLC; Frontier Communications of Pennsylvania, LLC; 
Frontier Communications of Seneca-Gorham, Inc.; Frontier Communications of the Southwest 
Inc.; Frontier Communications of Sylvan Lake, Inc.; Frontier Midstates Inc.; Frontier North Inc.; 
 
the presence of one of five categories of services as available to receive streamlined treatment.  
The services identified in this Application, which meet the requirements of the Adequate 
Replacement or Alternative Options Test, will also meet the newly adopted POTS replacement 
requirement.

<!-- page 71 -->
5 
Frontier Telephone of Rochester, Inc.; Frontier West Virginia Inc.; Ogden Telephone Company 
d/b/a Frontier Ogden Telephone Company; The Southern New England Telephone Company 
d/b/a Frontier Communications of Connecticut; Verizon Maryland LLC; Verizon New England 
Inc.; Verizon New York Inc.; Verizon North LLC; Verizon Pennsylvania LLC; and Verizon 
Washington, DC Inc., all Verizon companies, (collectively, “Verizon”), apply for authority under 
47 U.S.C. § 214(a) and 47 C.F.R. § 63.71 to discontinue their legacy TDM-based voice service 
over copper throughout portions of California, Connecticut, District of Columbia, Illinois, 
Maryland, Massachusetts, Michigan, New York, Pennsylvania, and West Virginia as specified 
herein.   
II.  
GRANTING THE APPLICATION ADVANCES THE BUILD AMERICA 
AGENDA.   
The Commission’s Build America Agenda is making it easier and faster for providers to 
retire copper and modernize U.S. communications infrastructure.7  Approval of this Application 
will advance the Build America Agenda and serve the public interest in multiple ways. 
First, approval will drive investment toward networks of the future and reduce the need 
to devote resources to infrastructure of the past.  Investment is essential to the Build America 
Agenda, which aims to “free up billions of dollars for new networks, instead of forcing providers 
to keep investing in old ones.”8  Keeping resources locked up in old copper-based POTS 
networks will thwart unleashing that investment. 
 
7 See Remarks of FCC Chairman Brendan Carr, “A Build Agenda for America,” Sioux Falls, 
South Dakota, (July 2, 2025), https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/ DOC-412663A1.pdf 
(“Build America Agenda”); see also Brendan Carr, Now Is the Time to Build, FCC BLOG (July 2, 
2025), https://www.fcc.gov/news-events/blog/2025/07/02/now-time-build; Inquiry Concerning 
Deployment of Advanced Telecommunications Capability to All Americans in a Reasonable and 
Timely Fashion, 2026 Section 706 Report, FCC 26-55, ¶¶ 65-67 (rel. Aug. 14, 2026) (“2026 
Section 706 Report”). 
8 Build America Agenda at 3.

<!-- page 72 -->
6 
In its Network Modernization Order, a unanimous, bipartisan Commission found that 
“the expansion of [ ] modern networks, and the benefits they afford, have been hindered by the 
need for carriers to divert important resources to the maintenance of aging and deteriorating 
legacy networks that deliver outdated services to an ever-decreasing number of subscribers.”9  
This includes copper-based networks, which the Commission recognized are “no longer 
sufficient to meet the bandwidth-hungry needs of modern communications and thus ha[ve] been 
rendered obsolete by more advanced networks.”10   
The cost of maintaining POTS networks has skyrocketed in recent years.  Verizon spends 
billions of dollars each year to maintain its legacy copper network.  These costs are enormous 
because of, among other things, rising costs of copper and the need for replacement parts that are 
increasingly difficult to procure as obsolete equipment is discontinued.11  Copper is also prone to 
theft,12 and a declining customer base makes copper more expensive to maintain on a per-
 
9 Network Modernization Order ¶ 2. 
10 Id. ¶ 9. 
11 See, e.g., Eric Fruits & Brian Albrecht, Paying to Stand Still: Legacy Copper Mandates in a 
Fiber World, INTERNATIONAL CENTER FOR LAW & ECONOMICS, at 12-13 (Feb. 27, 2026), 
https://laweconcenter.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/02/Copper-Retirement-Economics-v2-
2026.pdf (“ICLE Report”); Jonathan Spalter, Introducing America’s Connected Future, 
USTELECOM (Mar. 9, 2026), https://ustelecom.org/introducing-americas-connected-future/ 
(“USTelecom Article”) (“Service restoration can often take weeks if not months because 
replacement parts are harder to find as manufacturers discontinue declining product lines.”). 
12 See Protecting Critical Communications Infrastructure from Theft & Vandalism, USTELECOM, 
at 8 (Oct. 9, 2025), https://ustelecom.org/research/protecting-critical-communication-
infrastructure-report-fall-2025/ (reporting over 15,000 incidents of theft and vandalism between 
June 2024 – June 2025); Remarks of Commissioner Oliva Britt Trusty, “Copper Theft Crisis: 
Incident Management and Prosecutorial Collaboration Summit,” El Segundo, California (Oct. 7, 
2025), https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DOC-415046A1.pdf; Surveillance video shows 
two suspects after over $4,700 in copper cable, telecommunications equipment stolen, WSLS 
NEWS 10, https://www.wsls.com/news/local/2026/07/27/law-enforcement-searching-for-two-
suspects-after-over-4700-in-copper-cable-telecommunications-equipment-stolen/ (Aug. 3, 2026) 
(describing copper theft in Campbell County, Virginia); see also Sean Mahoney, New safeguards 
aren’t stopping copper thieves in Arizona, 13 NEWS (July 16, 2026),

<!-- page 73 -->
7 
customer basis13—meaning as time goes by each copper customer consumes disproportionately 
more resources.  Every dollar Verizon spends maintaining aging, little-used copper infrastructure 
diverts critical investment away from future-ready networks that customers across the country 
demand.    
By granting this Application, the Commission will help Verizon “transition [away] from 
legacy TDM-based networks,”14 allowing us to “invest more resources toward modernizing [our] 
networks so [] consumers can access more advanced communications services.”15  And granting 
this Application will allow the Commission to fulfill its goal of “accelerat[ing] the transition 
from aging copper” voice service “to the modern, high-speed” services “that Americans want 
and deserve.”16  This is a win for states and consumers alike, and it is fully consistent with 
Section 214, which Congress enacted to “protect Americans’ continued access to the nation’s 
communications networks while also preserving carriers’ ability to upgrade their services 
without the interruption of federal micromanaging.”17 
Second, granting this Application will allow Verizon to move away from the slow, 
inefficient copper-based services customers no longer want.  Last-mile copper connections to 
customer premises were designed for phone calls, not today’s heavy Internet usage.  As the 
Commission has recognized, copper-based networks’ “limited data bandwidth is no longer 
 
https://www.kold.com/2026/07/16/new-safeguards-arent-stopping-copper-thieves-arizona/ 
(describing string of copper thefts from street lights in South Tucson, Arizona). 
13 ICLE Report at 5, 14. 
14 Network Modernization Order ¶¶ 1, 3. 
15 Id. ¶ 2. 
16 Build America Agenda at 3. 
17 Accelerating Wireline Broadband Deployment by Removing Barriers to Infrastructure 
Investment, Report and Order, Declaratory Ruling, and Further Notice of Proposed Rulemaking, 
32 FCC Rcd 11128, ¶ 133 (2017).

<!-- page 74 -->
8 
sufficient to meet the bandwidth-hungry needs of modern communications.”18  Copper is also 
energy inefficient, particularly when compared to modern alternatives.19   
Not surprisingly, consumers have made clear that they do not want copper-based voice 
services anymore.  From 2014 to 2024, subscribers using copper last-mile connections fell 
nationwide by 81%.20  Almost four out of five U.S. adults use wireless-only for voice, with less 
than 4% of U.S. adults preferring landlines for phone calls.21  Today, wireless services account 
for roughly 83% of voice phone lines nationwide, with VoIP provided over modern broadband 
networks capturing most of the remainder.22  And the emergence of 5G home broadband and 
low-Earth orbit satellite services has led to meaningful new choices, including in areas that are 
difficult to reach by wired connections.23  Indeed, the Commission has found that “[h]igh-speed

see also ICLE Report at 10 (finding that transitioning the remaining 12.5 million copper 
subscribers to modern alternatives would produce estimated energy-cost savings of $398 million 
to $830 million, and noting Verizon data highlighting that fiber-delivered broadband is at least 
100 times more energy-efficient on a kWh-per-gigabyte basis than copper-delivered service). 
20 ICLE Report at 1, 4 (reporting that subscribers have decreased from nearly 66 million in 2014

https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/data/nhis/earlyrelease/wireless202506.pdf (2.3 percent of adults

22 Voice Telephone Services: Status as of June 30, 2025, FCC, INDUSTRY ANALYSIS DIVISION,

https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DOC-421558A1.pdf (“Voice Telephone Services 
Report”).  
23 Modernizing Spectrum Sharing for Satellite Broadband, Report and Order, FCC 26-26, at 
Statement of Chairman Brendan Carr (rel. May 1, 2026), 
https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DOC-421308A2.pdf (observing that “Americans today 
benefit from increased competition for their broadband dollars” because “[w]ireless carriers are

<!-- page 75 -->
9 
residential broadband—whether cable, fiber, or fixed wireless—has become more widely 
available” than ever before, and when factoring in satellite services, “appears to be virtually 
universally available to all Americans.”24   
In particular, consumers now regularly turn to fixed wireless service.  For example, in 
2024, 99% of new home broadband subscribers chose 5G for the third year in a row, with 3.7 
million additional 5G home subscriptions.25  And across the United States, fixed wireless access 
customers—particularly Verizon’s millions of wireless home customers—are the happiest with 
their service, while DSL copper customers are the unhappiest.26   
Just as copper voice reliance is small and declining nationwide, the same is true in the ten 
jurisdictions specifically at issue here.27  In Verizon’s case, use of its copper-based voice 
services continues to decrease, consistent with broader trends.  The overwhelming majority of 
consumers from the nine discontinuance states and the District of Columbia who could subscribe

Competition is driving this investment.  Verizon faces competition from a host of 
providers that have deployed modern communications technologies, including wireless and 
wireline, mobile and fixed, terrestrial and satellite, facilities-based and over-the-top.31  But we 
cannot invest optimally to modernize our networks and compete while continuing to devote huge 
sums of money to sustaining copper-based POTS.   
Last, Verizon will not leave customers behind.  Because it makes no sense to maintain a 
ubiquitous, costly legacy network that consumers have largely abandoned, we seek to transition 
customers to modern services provided by Verizon or another provider.  We will work to ensure 
that this is a smooth transition for copper-based POTS customers in the discontinuance area.  
Most affected customers who currently have copper POTS lines, sold as “Home Phone Service,” 
will have the option to buy voice service from Verizon that meets or exceeds their expectations 
in network performance and availability.  We are proud to have built the nation’s best wireless 
network, setting the highest bar for quality and reliability.32  Verizon’s award-winning national 
wireless network covers 99% of the U.S. population and supports approximately 147 million 
wireless retail connections,33 supporting mobile and fixed wireless services.34  Notably, 
Verizon’s mobile wireless service provides high-quality voice service as well as mobile 
broadband service at the vast majority of locations in the discontinuance area.35  Verizon also has

In this Application, we seek authority to discontinue copper services only where

In sum, this transition will leave voice customers well-served while unlocking resources 
for investment to expand and improve Verizon’s modern fiber and wireless networks.   
III.  
APPLICATION.  
 
Verizon seeks streamlined treatment and approval to discontinue copper-based POTS in 
the discontinuance area based on three showings.  First, as the Bureau has already found, 
Verizon mobile wireless service and fixed wireless legacy voice replacement services satisfy the 
Adequate Replacement Test.  Second, as the Bureau has recognized, the Alternative Options Test 
is satisfied in areas where Verizon Fios is available (i.e., where Verizon has deployed fiber).  
Third, consistent with precedent, the Adequate Replacement Test is satisfied in areas where any 
of ten leading third-party providers, such as AT&T, T-Mobile, Comcast, or Charter, offer 
facilities-based mobile wireless voice or cable- or fiber-based interconnected VoIP.39  
Collectively, these showings establish that Verizon is entitled to streamlined treatment and 
discontinuance authority for the entire discontinuance area, as defined above (i.e., qualifying 
locations in the ten jurisdictions at issue).40

A. 
Verizon’s Mobile Wireless Service and Fixed Wireless Legacy Voice 
Replacement Services Satisfy the Adequate Replacement Test. 
As detailed below, Verizon mobile wireless service41 and fixed wireless POTS voice 
replacement services42 are adequate replacements for Verizon POTS.  Verizon currently has 
approximately 46,000 customers using fixed wireless POTS voice replacement services and 
approximately 147 million wireless retail connections nationwide.  Verizon’s wireless network 
provides mobile wireless coverage throughout the United States—reaching more than 99% of the 
U.S. population.  Verizon’s mobile wireless and fixed wireless POTS voice replacement services 
comply with all applicable federal requirements and industry standards for 911 services, 
communications security, and services for individuals with disabilities.43  Verizon legacy voice 
replacement service customers can continue to use their existing POTS corded or cordless

1. Verizon’s Mobile Wireless and Fixed Wireless POTS Voice 
Replacement Services Offer Substantially Similar Levels of Network 
Performance and Availability as POTS. 
Based on the totality of the circumstances, Verizon’s mobile wireless and fixed wireless 
POTS voice replacement services have substantially similar network performance and 
availability as the POTS Verizon would discontinue. 
a. 
Verizon’s Network Performance. 
Under the “network performance” prong of the Adequate Replacement Test, an applicant 
seeking to discontinue service can make “a demonstration, based on the totality of the 
circumstances,” that the network on which a replacement service will run provides substantially 
similar performance and availability.48  The totality of the circumstances shows that users can 
rely on Verizon’s mobile wireless network for mobile wireless service and fixed wireless POTS 
voice replacement services to replace the legacy voice services Verizon seeks to discontinue.  
Verizon offers a 4G LTE and 5G NR (“New Radio”) mobile broadband and voice service at the 
vast majority of locations in Verizon’s service areas in California, Connecticut, District of 
Columbia, Illinois, Maryland, Massachusetts, Michigan, New York, Pennsylvania, and West 
Virginia.  This same mobile wireless network enables Verizon’s fixed wireless POTS voice 
replacement services.   
In its recently approved discontinuance applications, Verizon detailed the technical 
analysis, third-party measurements, and nationwide awards demonstrating that its wireless 
network performs exceptionally well.49  These technical facts and measurements have not 
changed, they apply here, and Verizon incorporates them into this Application.  Awards specific

support voice service throughout the discontinuance areas in these states where it offers mobile

Because customer calls made with Verizon’s fixed wireless POTS voice replacement service are 
carried using the same network and equipment as Verizon’s wireless customers’ VoLTE calls, 
they will experience the same service availability.  In addition, Verizon’s legacy voice 
replacement customer premises devices can remain positioned in the optimal area of a 
customer’s residence or premises, which means Verizon’s legacy voice replacement services 
provide reliable wireless voice service that, in some cases, exceeds the performance of mobile 
wireless services. 
c. 
Verizon’s Replacement Service Coverage.

streamlined treatment demonstrate that either: “(i) a single replacement service reaches the entire 
geographic footprint of the service area subject to discontinuance; or (ii) there are multiple 
providers who collectively cover the entirety of the [discontinuance] area.”92  As reflected in 
both the National Broadband Map and Verizon’s coverage map, Verizon’s LTE network is

modern cell phones can go without a charge for more than 24 hours when kept in standby mode.  
For longer outages, modern cell phones can be recharged in a car, with a laptop, or with a power 
bank, which is inexpensive and available online and at various retail stores.  The customer 
premises equipment for Verizon’s fixed wireless POTS voice replacement service includes

b. 
Communications Security. 
Verizon maintains a comprehensive and mature cybersecurity program designed to 
protect the confidentiality, integrity, and availability of its systems, networks, and information, 
including for its wireless services, through administrative, technical, and physical safeguards.  
Grounded in the National Institute of Standards and Technology Cybersecurity Framework, the 
enterprise-wide program incorporates robust policies, strategic planning, cutting-edge 
technology, comprehensive training, and a culture of continuous improvement to proactively 
identify and protect against cybersecurity risks, and to enable rapid detection, response, and 
recovery from incidents.  Verizon employs more than one thousand cybersecurity professionals 
who establish and oversee security strategies and policies and partner with business units to 
implement appropriate security measures and protect customer data.  Verizon’s cybersecurity 
ecosystem—subject to review with key stakeholders at least once every year to ensure alignment 
with current regulations and business practices—reflects its commitment to providing effective 
protections against cyber threats.  Verizon therefore certifies that its network security 
management practices are enterprise-wide and that it safeguards the availability, integrity, and 
confidentiality of the network without differentiation between services, geographic areas, or

Beyond Verizon’s specific practices, the proposed transition is consistent with 
communications security generally because, among other things, “TDM cannot support many 
software-based cybersecurity tools that protect modern data and voice infrastructure.”99  Further, 
“for TDM networks . . . in many cases, parts in use today have been running since the 1990s and 
are reaching the end of their lifespan.  OEMs have also stopped producing certain parts, so when 
replacements are needed, operators [such as Verizon] must turn to the resale market. . . . [I]t is 
difficult to track the origin of resale equipment and verify whether it meets security 
standards.”100  And “[a]dvanced ‘frontier’ AI models have raised the threat level of reliance on 
TDM network architectures drastically” because “TDM networks do not have the agility to 
defend against” those threats in the same manner as modern networks.101  In sum, “the 
replacement service offers comparably effective protection”—indeed, better protection—“from 
network security risks.”102

Commission precedent supports this conclusion.  While the discontinuance rules and 
associated information collections adopted in the Network Modernization Order are not yet 
effective,116 the order text, including the Commission’s conclusions regarding adequate

VoIP service is an adequate replacement” and that “facilities-based mobile wireless service 
operating at speeds of at least 5/1 Mbps” is “an adequate replacement.”118  The Commission 
reiterated, consistent with the preexisting Adequate Replacement Test, that “[p]ermitting a 
discontinuing carrier to rely on the availability of an adequate replacement service offered by a 
third party to support a technology transitions discontinuance application and to rely on publicly 
available information to make the requisite showing is consistent with previous Commission 
action.”119   
The recent Network Modernization Order represents the Commission’s definitive, 
unanimous, and bipartisan view on the topic of adequate replacements.  Nevertheless, it is far 
from the only precedent supporting the conclusion that the Providers’ fiber, cable, and mobile 
wireless-based voice services constitute adequate replacements for copper-based POTS.  Fiber,

1. The Providers Offer Substantially Similar Levels of Network 
Performance and Availability as Verizon POTS. 
Based on the totality of the circumstances, the Providers offer substantially similar 
network infrastructure and service quality as the POTS Verizon would discontinue.   
a. 
Network Performance and Availability. 
The totality of the circumstances demonstrates that users can rely on the Providers for 
substantially similar network performance and availability as Verizon POTS.126  Indeed, the 
cable, fiber, and mobile technologies employed by the Providers are far superior to legacy 
copper.  The Commission found almost two years ago that “[a]lthough the public switched 
telephone network was once the only means to connect, there now exists a multitude of other 
voice service options for consumers in the United States.”127  The Network Modernization Order 
confirmed specifically at the Commission level that facilities-based interconnected VoIP and 
facilities-based 4G LTE (5/1 Mbps) or faster mobile wireless are adequate replacements,128 
reenforcing earlier Bureau discontinuance determinations, as discussed above.  These decisions 
represent a clear-cut conclusion that facilities-based interconnected VoIP delivered over cable or 
fiber and facilities-based mobile wireless voice meet consumers’ needs. 
The marketplace reinforces this sound conclusion.  As detailed in Section II above, most 
consumers choose mobile wireless for voice service, and most of the remainder pick 
interconnected VoIP.  From a technological perspective, voice requires minimal network
